- Collaborations on YouTube: Kind: captions Language: en Collaborations make it easier to showcase&nbsp; how you work with other creators on YouTube&nbsp;&nbsp; and grow your channels together. You can add&nbsp; up to five collaborators on a single video,&nbsp;&nbsp; which will feature each creator's name and&nbsp; a subscribe button for all channels. The&nbsp;&nbsp; video will appear in the subscriptions feed of&nbsp; all listed collaborators audiences and may be&nbsp;&nbsp; recommended to them, increasing discoverability.&nbsp; Let's walk through how to use collaborations and&nbsp;&nbsp; some important things to keep in mind when using&nbsp; them. To invite other creators to a collaboration&nbsp;&nbsp; on your videos in Shorts, head to YouTube Studio&nbsp; on desktop or mobile. During the upload flow&nbsp;&nbsp; under the details page, choose show more, then&nbsp; invite a collaborator. Search for the channel&nbsp;&nbsp; you want to add. You can also choose whether&nbsp; to grant the collaborator analytics viewing.&nbsp;&nbsp; Note that even if you grant this access, they&nbsp; won't be able to see your revenue information.&nbsp;&nbsp; Then choose create link. Here you can share the&nbsp; link with the person you want to collaborate&nbsp;&nbsp; with. The link will be active after you&nbsp; upload your video. You can invite up to&nbsp;&nbsp; five channels to collaborate with. You'll be&nbsp; able to see when a collaborator has accepted&nbsp;&nbsp; your invitation and use the three dot menu&nbsp; to reshare the link, manage analytics access,&nbsp;&nbsp; or remove the collaborator. Once your video&nbsp; is uploaded in the invited channels accept&nbsp;&nbsp; the invite link, the video or short will display&nbsp; all collaborators channel names with a subscribe&nbsp;&nbsp; button for each. To feature your collaborations&nbsp; on your channel homepage, head to YouTube Studio,&nbsp;&nbsp; then customization, then home tab. Choose&nbsp; add section, then choose collaborations. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; let's walk through some important things to&nbsp; keep in mind when using collaborations. First,&nbsp;&nbsp; even when you add collaborators, you're still&nbsp; in control of the video or short. Only you can&nbsp;&nbsp; make changes to the video. You'll also retain&nbsp; full ownership. As the owner, you'll keep all&nbsp;&nbsp; the revenue, views, and watch time from the video.&nbsp; And remember, if you have paid product placements,&nbsp;&nbsp; endorsements, or sponsorships, you'll need&nbsp; to check the paid promotions box. For more&nbsp;&nbsp; info on using collaborations on your videos and&nbsp; shorts, check out the links in the description.

- How To Collab With Other YouTubers: Kind: captions Language: en - I'm gonna tell you a few different ways that you can do collaborations with other people, even if they don't live anywhere near you. And we're starting right now. (rap music) What is going on? My name is Nick, welcome to another video. If this is your first time here, and you wanna learn how to grow your channel, make videos, and all types of other Youtube related stuff, start now, by subscribing and clicking the bell so you don't miss anything. And real quick, let me know if you have ever done a collaboration with somebody that doesn't live anywhere near you, and let me know how it went down in the comments. Okay, collaborations are a fantastic way to grow your channel. Basically the idea is you are putting yourself in front of an audience that would not have seen you otherwise, and then the other person that you're collaborating with, they're putting themselves in front of your audience, who your audience might not have seen otherwise as well. Collaborations are extremely powerful, and depending on who they could make a big impact on your channel. But sometimes let's say that you want to collaborate with somebody that lives in another state, or they live in another country, or they live completely on another side of the planet, how do you do it? The first way to pull off that kind of collaboration is you get on Skype, and you have the person that you're collaborating with get on Skype. You both set up your cameras just like you would right here with this whole thing, you both set up your cameras, they record their part, you record your part, while you're staring into the lens, but you're actually having the conversation over Skype. So you have the audio of them speaking, they have the audio of you speaking, but you're actually recording and looking into the camera. Once you have the recording part of that finished then you would upload it to your Dropbox account, they would upload it to their Dropbox account, and then you would basically exchange the footage that way through sharing a link to that Dropbox folder. Super easy, super fast, done. The next way that you can do a long distance collaboration is basically each of you fill in the body content for the video, so basically the main part of the video, all the stuff that's in the middle of it, you record that part for them, they record that part for you. The same exact thing applies as in the first part of this, where you would then edit and upload those clips to Dropbox. And then once you download those clips, you basically do the introduction to the video, and then you hand it off to them. And then you come in at the end of the video to kinda tie everything up, make the recommendation for their channel, and kinda close out your video like you normally would. The important thing about doing it this way is the actual handoff. So basically what you would do is you would pass it to that other person. So just like you would do in a conversation style video where you would say, "Oh hey, whatever your name is, how are you today?" And then they would come in and say, "Oh, I'm doing great, thanks." You would do that same basic thing, but basically you would have them start out their video with a, "Oh thanks for having me on your channel, whatever your name is." And then they would go into the body content, and then before the end of their segment, they would pass it back to you by saying, "Thanks again for having me on your channel, "whatever your name is, back to you." And then from there you pick it up where they left off. And then you say, "Oh, it was great, loved having you on. "It was such a great bit of information that you shared, "Thank you so much." And then you go in to closing out your video. Another way that you can do it, and this all depends on the quality of the content that you're used to putting out, and that your viewers are used to seeing, that's not as high quality as exchanging the footage. Is you can actually just hop on a interview. You can use a Peer.in as an example, or you can use Zoom, or you can use even Skype really, or Google Hangouts, to where basically there's more than one person on the screen, and you basically just have that conversation back and forth. And then you record it using Camtasia, or another screen recorder. If you're using Zoom, you can actually do that screen recording inside of Zoom and it'll actually upload to their cloud, then you can download it. Or you can even record it straight to your computer. Doing it that way definitely gets the job done, it might not be the highest quality, but it's definitely an audience exchange, to where you get a chance to be in front of each others audiences, and you know, it can also be effective. One other way that you can do this is the way that you make it extremely easy for the person that you're collaborating with. And that's basically where you do the whole entire thing as a guest host. The idea behind this one is you do everything, like they would normally do on their channels, except you do it as you instead of them. So let's say as an example, if you were gonna collaborate with me on my channel, you would actually be that person on the video right when the video first opens up. That can be a little uncomfortable for an audience but since it is on the channel, they might stick around just to see what it is that's going on. - The Importance of Thumbnails: Kind: captions Language: en Thumbnails are super important, guys. And I’m not talking about the ones that entice you to click on our videos. I’m talking about these thumbnails. Because when you’re an animal with thumbs, sometimes claws just won’t do. And some animals only have nails on their thumbs, because they’re just that helpful. But exactly how you and I&nbsp; started growing thumbnails, or any nails really, is still a mystery. So let’s dive into what&nbsp; scientists have uncovered so far. [INTRO] Let’s start with a bit of Nail 101. The Latin name for a nail is an ungula. Not to be confused with unguis, the general term for any kind of&nbsp; thing protruding out of the digits: claw, hoof, nail, et cetera. And claws, nails, and hooves&nbsp; do all share some basic parts. For example, they all contain&nbsp; a protein called keratin, which gives them their toughness. But nails are unique because they feature a flat&nbsp; plate of keratinized skin that sits on top of a digit and&nbsp; then kind of juts out from there. Hooves are a little special in their own way, because most of the animal’s&nbsp; weight is bearing down on them. Because of that, the plate grows at an angle and wraps around most of the tip of the digit. It also means the keratinized&nbsp; bit has to be thicker. And claws? Well, if you’ve ever been unfortunate enough to be at the receiving end of a cat swipe, you’ll know they’re longer,&nbsp; and more compressed than nails. For the purpose of this video, we’re going to stick to just nails and claws… which thanks to their different forms,&nbsp; are better suited for different tasks. For example, since they’re pointy, claws are great for grabbing a thing and helping that thing rest&nbsp; in the hand…paw…whatever. If you’re a predator, that “thing” might be prey. If you’re something like a sloth, it could be a fat, bark-covered tree branch. But nails might be a better&nbsp; option if you want to be stealthy. Why let claws loudly scratch up that tree branch… risking your prey hearing you before&nbsp; you’re close enough to strike… when you can evolve much quieter nails? And speaking of branches, if the ones you keep reaching for are so slim you can wrap your digits around them, then claws are a pain in the butt&nbsp; because they’ll dig into your palm. Or should I say pain in the leg,&nbsp; because Hank tells me butt is legs… Whether or not what I just said has&nbsp; got you curling your own fingers into your palm right now, try touching something with your fingertip. Our nails can also help make&nbsp; our fingertips more sensitive, because they give the skin under&nbsp; the nail something to push against. This extra sensitivity also makes handling&nbsp; and manipulating small objects easier. But let’s get back to these guys . You probably already have an&nbsp; inkling that having a thumb digit… also known as the pollex…is super handy. Especially an opposable one But it turns out some animals might get similar&nbsp; benefits from having just the thumb nail. In 2025, a team of scientists retraced the&nbsp; evolution of thumbnails in one very particular, but also very massive, animal group: rodents. If it surprises you to learn that rodents&nbsp; have thumbnails, you wouldn’t be alone. Even other scientists have&nbsp; kind of dismissed the rodent&nbsp;&nbsp; thumbnail as a vestigial or non-functional unguis. And that’s partly because the&nbsp; rest of their digits end in claws. It’d be weird to have one&nbsp; unguis for all your fingers and a different unguis for&nbsp; just your thumbs, right? Well, this research team dove into the Field&nbsp; Museum’s massive collection of rodent skins, and looked at 433 different&nbsp; genus groups to catalogue which rodents actually had thumbnails. They also noted where all these&nbsp; rodents preferred to live… like in trees or underground… as well as whether they used their&nbsp; mouths or hands to handle food. From there, the team could&nbsp; reconstruct a family tree to see how and when thumbnails&nbsp; popped up in evolutionary history. It turns out a whopping 86 percent&nbsp; of rodents have thumbnails… at least of the ones sampled. But they all likely shared a&nbsp; thumbnail-wielding common ancestor. As the descendants of that ancestor evolved into the many different species we see today, sometimes that thumbnail turned into a claw… and sometimes it was just lost altogether! After all, sometimes thumbnails&nbsp; might be less helpful than a couple more claws. For example, most rodents with&nbsp; thumbclaws live below ground where they’d need to dig. But there are a ton of rodent species, from squirrels, to beavers, to&nbsp; everyone’s favorite, the capybara. They’re the most abundant kind of mammal, found in all sorts of habitats around the world. And according to this research team, thumbnails may have had a part to play in that. Specifically, because they provided&nbsp; access to a super exclusive food source. Nuts are chock full of energy, but&nbsp; getting at that energy is a challenge. Opening and eating them requires&nbsp; a good amount of dexterity. So if you evolve yourself some&nbsp; thumbnails to improve your dexterity, you might be able to access&nbsp; nutrients your neighbors can’t. And if you’re hankering for nutrients right now, it’s a great time to grab some, because it’s time for an ad break. This SciShow video is supported by DeleteMe, the hands-free subscription service&nbsp; that removes your personal information from hundreds of data broker&nbsp; and people search sites. DeleteMe works to keep your&nbsp; personal information personal and reduce your risk of identity theft, scams, phishing&nbsp; attempts, harassment, and doxing. And not just your info. DeleteMe has been the leading expert in personally identifiable information removal for over 15 years and was recently named&nbsp; the #1 data-removal service by Wirecutter. So /lots/ of people trust them. Like our executive producer,&nbsp; Nicole, who just signed up! And said they got to work&nbsp; removing her info super quickly. They’ve already reviewed over 900&nbsp; records and begun removing them. Plus these privacy advisors are real&nbsp; people available for custom requests and help when you need it. Just use the QR code on screen&nbsp; or go to joindeleteme.com/SCISHOW and use promo code SCISHOW at checkout. You’ll get 20% off DeleteMe US consumer plans. Alright, so rodents probably started out with&nbsp; digits rocking mostly claws and two thumbnails. But what about primates? What about me? Did humanity also start out with&nbsp; just one thumbnail on each hand, and then somewhere along the line&nbsp; swap everything else to match? Well, it’s complicated. Like many of our rodent friends, many living primates also&nbsp; have an odd-one-out digit. Although in our case, that&nbsp; digit is on our hind feet… which for primates with actual&nbsp; hands, are just called feet. That arrangement might sound weird to a human, because all our digits have nails. But some New World Monkeys only&nbsp; have nails on each big toe, a.k.a. the hallux. The rest are claws. Meanwhile, Madagascan lemurs are&nbsp; rocking kinda the opposite setup. Most digits on their hind feet end in a nail, but the toe next to each&nbsp; hallux has a specialized claw. They use it for cleaning themselves, so humans call it a grooming claw. So what did the ancestor of all primates have? And how did humanity’s&nbsp; “oops all nails” come about? We don’t know. One hypothesis says primates&nbsp; started out with mostly claws, which came in a variety of shapes and helped us scurry up thick tree branches. Then, as they started to use their palms more… for moving objects around&nbsp; and eventually using tools… those claws broadened and flattened into nails. However, in a paper published back in 1999, one research group concluded&nbsp; the ancestor to modern primates had already lost all its claws&nbsp; except for the grooming claw. They argued that early primates&nbsp; probably lost the deepest, hardest layer of their claws, which meant the claws were less&nbsp; strong and the tips could be worn away as primates moved around more on the ground. Then in 2018, a paper building off that work&nbsp; tackled the evolution of that grooming claw. The study looked at animals called omomyoids, a group of early primates&nbsp; that kinda looked like a mix between lemurs and tarsiers. Researchers think that as very early primates evolved more developed big toes… specifically, big toes that could&nbsp; help them climb more easily… their second toes were freed up for other uses. And since keeping parasites off&nbsp; yourself is a good way to stay healthy, grooming claws might have been one of those uses. So around 56 million years ago, our ancestors were probably&nbsp; rocking nails and one claw. Then, as some primates became more&nbsp; social and could groom each other, they swapped that claw for another nail. And if you’re something like a titi monkey, you became too solitary and had to&nbsp; re-evolve a grooming claw to DIY. So scientists have these sort of snapshots&nbsp; of what primate claws or nails looked like at different times. But it’s still not clear exactly how… or even really when… we went from a diversity of claws,&nbsp; to potentially just a grooming claw, to the nails we have today. For now, the best idea we have is that&nbsp; somehow claws became a disadvantage, either because our ancestors&nbsp; weren’t climbing thick tree trunks, or were spending more time on the ground. We might not know exactly&nbsp; how our thumbnails evolved. But if you want to check out the&nbsp; evolution of SciShow’s thumbnails, you can go explore that for yourself. [ OUTRO ]

So, if you're starting out or if you have a small channel for a while, whatever you do, do not pretend to be an expert. Instead, you need to lean into being a beginner. People will love it if you share your struggles. Like, share what you're learning and share as you go. And then we have your journey. And instead of hiding the fact you're a new channel, make it part of your brand. Document your first 90 days. Document your first 100 subscribers. Document your first dollar online. These milestones create a journey your audience can follow. And when your audience feels like they're there with you on your journey, they'll hit that subscribe button so they don't miss what happens next. And when you create content that connects with people, your videos stop just being something to watch and start becoming like something to follow. And that's the difference between a viewer and a subscriber. Now the fourth method is social proof. And this is something that every small YouTuber seriously underestimates. Now social proof is the idea that people judge how valuable or trustworthy something is based on how popular it already looks. Now in YouTube terms that means that if your video only has like five views, unfortunately most people won't click on it. Or let's say if your channel only has like 20 subscribers, most people will assume that you're not worth watching. It's unfair, I know, but it's reality. But here's the thing. As a new YouTuber or a small YouTuber, you actually have an advantage here because people's expectations of you are already low. And that gives you an opportunity to overd deliver. And what I mean by this is when people click on a small channel and the content is way better than they expected, you instantly stand out because your viewers will feel like they've discovered you and they're far more likely to subscribe because of that. So, the key here is to lean into your authenticity. Don't try and be the guru who knows it all. Like, don't pretend you're bigger than you are. Instead, be transparent about your journey and make your viewers feel like they're growing with you. Now, for me, at the start of my journey on this channel, I literally called myself a small YouTuber in all of my videos. Like, all the way up till I hit about 50,000 subscribers. And it worked because it made me relatable. My viewers weren't just watching my videos. They felt like they were part of my story. So instead of letting low views and low subscribers discourage you, use them as part of your positioning. Show them that you're learning, improving, and growing, and invite your audience to grow with you. That's how you flip social proof from a disadvantage into a superpower. And then finally, method five is retention and call to actions. Now, your retention rate is probably the single most important metric on YouTube because your retention rate is basically how long people stay watching your videos. And YouTube only really cares about one thing. Are people sticking around and watching all of the video or are they clicking off early? Because the longer people watch your video for, the more your video gets pushed out to more and more people. However, the less time people spend watching your video, the less YouTube will push it. So, how do you increase retention? Well, the first 30 seconds are absolutely critical. Every retention graph will look like this. A huge drop off of people almost instantly. So, to improve your overall retention rate, the easiest thing you can do is to make sure you hook the viewer in straight away, within the first 30 seconds. This means that your opening scene can't have any fluff, no boring intros, and no rambling. Just get straight to the value. And then another tactic I use in my own videos is teasing. Now, what I mean by this is if you're going to drop a big insight or something super interesting later in the video, tell people up front. literally just be like, "Stick around until the end because I'm going to show you the exact strategy I use to get 50,000 subscribers from one video." And that's what's called future pacing. It basically perks up interest and gives people a reason to keep watching. But retention doesn't stop with the content itself. It ties directly into your call to actions. And most small channels mess this up by saying, "Please like this video and subscribe to my channel within the first 10 to 30 seconds." But the truth is, people don't care about subscribing unless you actually give them a reason to. So instead, what you need to do is give them as much value as possible and then ask them to subscribe to you. And then tie this to the value you gave them. For example, what you could do is say something like, "If you want more strategies, like this one that got me 50,000 new subscribers, make sure to subscribe so you don't miss the next video." Like something like that. And that way subscribing feels like the natural next step for them, like not a favor to you. Master both retention and call to actions, and I promise you, your growth will explode. Now, if you want to take your YouTube channel to the next step, I have a number of different YouTube growth courses that start from just $59. Now, my YouTube Academy is incredibly popular right now, but I also have just released the YouTube Wealth Accelerator, a cheaper course, but full of a ton of value that will teach you all you need to know about YouTube growth. The link is in the description below, so come and join us. If not, YouTube thinks that this is the most suitable video for you to watch next.

- Daily Habits of Successful People | Brian Tracy: Kind: captions Language: en Hello, I'm Brian Tracy and I'm always being asked what are the habits of successful people? What is it that successful people do habitually every single day that leads to them being in the top 20% of money earners in our society? Well, Aristotle said that 95% of everything you do is the result of habit. So the rule is form good habits and make them your masters rather than allowing bad habits to form. In fact the other rule says this is that good habits are hard to form but easy to live with. Bad habits are easy to form but hard to live with. Now one of the turning points in my life and my studies of psychology was the discovery that all habits are learned and can be unlearned. Actually, you don't actually unlearn a habit. You simply replace a bad habit with a good habit that has more powerful power and impact. And how do you develop a good habit? You develop a habit by repetition. Almost everything you do from the time you get up in the morning is habit. So, start to think about yourself what would be the best habits to have. Now, I have written a book called Million-Dollar Habits. It has 12 chapters, 12 critical areas of life. And in each chapter, there's 20 or 30 habits. So, let me give you some of the best. Number one, develop the habit of daily goal setting and goal orientation. What does this mean? Is when you go to sleep at night, think about your goals for the coming day. Make a list for each day of all the goals, the little goals you're going to accomplish that day. Keep a journal and write down your goals on a regular basis. When you're driving through the day, think about your goals. When you're talking to other people, think about your goals. You see, you do become what you think about most of the time. You do achieve what you think about most of the time. So, all successful people and all wealthy people think about their goals most of the time. Second of all, be resultsoriented. Now, resultsoriented people are always thinking of the most important things that they can do right now to achieve their most important results. Successful people make a list of everything they have to do and before they start working, they set priorities on the list. Here's a great way to become resultoriented. Actually, it's life-changing. Ask yourself once you have your list for the day, if I could only do one thing on this list before I was called out of town for a month, what one activity would I want to be sure to get completed? And put a circle around that and start on that activity immediately first thing. and then discipline yourself to work non-stop until you complete that one activity. This simple technique has to taken people from rags to riches all over the world for at least a hundred years. Pick your most important task and start on it immediately and then discipline yourself to stay with it until it's complete. Another habit that you can establish is to become people oriented. Recognize that everything that you achieve in life is going to be with the help or support or cooperation of other people. So always think what is it that other people want and need from me? How can I help other people so they'll want to help me back? If you're in sales top sales people think about their customers all the time. They think about who their customers are and what their customers want and how they can help them the most and how they can help them even more today. In their relationships, successful people are very focused on the most important people in their worlds, both personal and in business. Another habit you can have is to stay health oriented. Think about your health most of the time. Think about eating less food and better food. Think about daily exercise. is thinking about getting lots of rest.   15. @Breathflowconnection: The one habit that changed everything for me was starting the day with deliberate breath control before looking at a screen. Not meditation in the traditional sense. Just five minutes of slow breathing at a rate of about 5.5 breaths per minute (5.5 seconds in, 5.5 seconds out). Research shows this specific cadence maximizes heart rate variability and blood flow efficiency. It is the same rhythm found in rosary prayers, Buddhist chants, and Japanese, African, and Hawaiian breathing traditions. They all converge on this exact tempo independently. What this does practically is shift your nervous system from reactive mode to responsive mode before the day even starts. Most people wake up and immediately flood their system with cortisol by checking email or social media. Five minutes of controlled breathing sets a completely different baseline for how you process stress for the rest of the day. I have been facilitating breathwork sessions for six years and this is the single simplest thing I recommend to everyone. No app. No equipment. Just your lungs and a timer.

- What makes something go viral? | Dao Nguyen: Kind: captions Language: en Last year, some BuzzFeed employees were scheming to prank their boss, Ze Frank, on his birthday. They decided to put a family of baby goats in his office. (Laughter) Now, BuzzFeed had recently signed on to the Facebook Live experiment, and so naturally, we decided to livestream the whole event on the internet to capture the moment when Ze would walk in and discover livestock in his office. We thought the whole thing would last maybe 10 minutes, and a few hundred company employees would log in for the inside joke. But what happened? Ze kept on getting delayed: he went to get a drink, he was called to a meeting, the meeting ran long, he went to the bathroom. More and more people started logging in to watch the goats. By the time Ze walked in more than 30 minutes later, 90,000 viewers were watching the livestream. Now, our team had a lot of discussion about this video and why it was so successful. It wasn't the biggest live video that we had done to date. The biggest one that we had done involved a fountain of cheese. But it performed so much better than we had expected. What was it about the goats in the office that we didn't anticipate? Now, a reasonable person could have any number of hypotheses. Maybe people love baby animals. Maybe people love office pranks. Maybe people love stories about their bosses or birthday surprises. But our team wasn't really thinking about what the video was about. We were thinking about what the people watching the video were thinking and feeling. We read some of the 82,000 comments that were made during the video, and we hypothesized that they were excited because they were participating in the shared anticipation of something that was about to happen. They were part of a community, just for an instant, and it made them happy. So we decided that we needed to test this hypothesis. What could we do to test this very same thing? The following week, armed with the additional knowledge that food videos are very popular, we dressed two people in hazmat suits and wrapped rubber bands around a watermelon until it exploded. Eight hundred thousand people watched the 690th rubber band explode the watermelon, marking it as the biggest Facebook Live event to date. The question I get most frequently is: How do you make something go viral? The question itself is misplaced; it's not about the something. It's about what the people doing the something, reading or watching -- what are they thinking? Now, most media companies, when they think about metadata, they think about subjects or formats. It's about goats, it's about office pranks, it's about food, it's a list or a video or a quiz, it's 2,000 words long, it's 15 minutes long, it has 23 embedded tweets or 15 images. Now, that kind of metadata is mildly interesting, but it doesn't actually get at what really matters. What if, instead of tagging what articles or videos are about, what if we asked: How is it helping our users do a real job in their lives? Last year, we started a project to formally categorize our content in this way. We called it, "cultural cartography." It formalized an informal practice that we've had for a really long time: don't just think about the subject matter; think also about, and in fact, primarily about, the job that your content is doing for the reader or the viewer. Let me show you the map that we have today. Each bubble is a specific job, and each group of bubbles in a specific color are related jobs. First up: humor. "Makes me laugh." There are so many ways to make somebody laugh. You can be laughing at someone, you could laugh at specific internet humor, you could be laughing at some good, clean, inoffensive dad jokes. "This is me." Identity. People are increasingly using media to explain, "This is who I am. This is my upbringing, this is my culture, this is my fandom, this is my guilty pleasure, and this is how I laugh about myself." "Helps me connect with another person." This is one of the greatest gifts of the internet. It's amazing when you find a piece of media that precisely describes your bond with someone. This is the group of jobs that helps me do something -- helps me settle an argument, helps me learn something about myself or another person, or helps me explain my story. that makes me feel something -- makes me curious or sad or restores my faith in humanity. Many media companies and creators do put themselves in their audiences' shoes. But in the age of social media, we can go much farther. People are connected to each other on Facebook, on Twitter, and they're increasingly using media to have a conversation and to talk to each other. If we can be a part of establishing a deeper connection between two people, then we will have done a real job for these people. Let me give you some examples of how this plays out. This is one of my favorite lists: "32 Memes You Should Send Your Sister Immediately" -- immediately. For example, "When you're going through your sister's stuff, and you hear her coming up the stairs." Absolutely, I've done that. "Watching your sister get in trouble for something that you did and blamed on her." Yes, I've done that as well. This list got three million views. Why is that? Because it did, very well, several jobs: "This is us." "Connect with family." Here are some of the thousands and thousands of comments that sisters sent to each other using this list. Sometimes we discover what jobs do after the fact. This quiz, "Pick an Outfit and We'll Guess Your Exact Age and Height," went very viral: 10 million views. Ten million views. I mean -- did we actually determine the exact age and height of 10 million people? That's incredible. It's incredible. In fact, we didn't. Turns out that this quiz went extremely viral among a group of 55-and-up women -- who were surprised and delighted that BuzzFeed determined that they were 28 and 5'9". "They put me at 34 years younger and seven inches taller. I dress for comfort and do not give a damn what anyone says. Age is a state of mind." This quiz was successful not because it was accurate, but because it allowed these ladies to do a very important job -- the humblebrag. Now, we can even apply this framework to recipes and food. A recipe's normal job is to tell you what to make for dinner or for lunch. And this is how you would normally brainstorm for a recipe: you figure out what ingredients you want to use, what recipe that makes, and then maybe you slap a job on at the end to sell it. But what if we flipped it around and thought about the job first? One brainstorming session involved the job of bonding. So, could we make a recipe that brought people together? This is not a normal brainstorming process at a food publisher. So we know that people like to bake together, and we know that people like to do challenges together, so we decided to come up with a recipe that involved those two things, and we challenged ourselves: Could we get people to say, "Hey, BFF, let's see if we can do this together"? The resulting video was the "Fudgiest Brownies Ever" video. It was enormously successful in every metric possible -- 70 million views. And people said the exact things that we were going after: "Hey, Colette, we need to make these, are you up for a challenge?" "Game on." It did the job that it set out to do, which was to bring people together over baking and chocolate. I'm really excited about the potential for this project. When we talk about this framework with our content creators, they instantly get it, no matter what beat they cover, what country they’re in, or what language they speak. So cultural cartography has helped us massively scale our workforce training. When we talk about this project and this framework with advertisers and brands, they also instantly get it, because advertisers, more often than media companies, understand how important it is to understand the job that their products are doing for customers. But the reason I'm the most excited about this project is because it changes the relationship between media and data. Most media companies think of media as "mine." How many fans do I have? How many followers have I gained? How many views have I gotten? How many unique IDs do I have in my data warehouse? But that misses the true value of data, which is that it's yours. If we can capture in data what really matters to you, and if we can understand more the role that our work plays in your actual life, the better content we can create for you, and the better that we can reach you. Who are you? How did you get there? Where are you going? What do you care about? What can you teach us? That's cultural cartography. Thank you. (Applause)

06:44 🎯 Viral success was also seen with a quiz offering inaccurate personal results, appealing for its surprise and identity play.
07:12 👭 Content that facilitates bonding, like baking challenges, achieves high engagement, evidenced by the success of the "Fudgiest Brownies Ever" video.
08:49 🌍 Cultural cartography helps BuzzFeed's creators and advertisers understand content's role and maximize engagement.
09:53 🔍 The project aims to use data for creating meaningful content that captures the actual needs and experiences of its audience.

- How changing your story can change your life | Lori Gottlieb | TED: Kind: captions Language: en I'm going to start by telling you about an email that I saw in my inbox recently. Now, I have a pretty unusual inbox because I'm a therapist and I write an advice column called "Dear Therapist," so you can imagine what's in there. I mean, I've read thousands of very personal letters from strangers all over the world. And these letters range from heartbreak and loss, to spats with parents or siblings. I keep them in a folder on my laptop, and I've named it "The Problems of Living." So, I get this email, I get lots of emails just like this, and I want to bring you into my world for a second and read you one of these letters. And here's how it goes. "Dear Therapist, I've been married for 10 years and things were good until a couple of years ago. That's when my husband stopped wanting to have sex as much, and now we barely have sex at all." I'm sure you guys were not expecting this. (Laughter) "Well, last night I discovered that for the past few months, he's been secretly having long, late-night phone calls with a woman at his office. I googled her, and she's gorgeous. I can't believe this is happening. My father had an affair with a coworker when I was young and it broke our family apart. Needless to say, I'm devastated. If I stay in this marriage, I'll never be able to trust my husband again. But I don't want to put our kids through a divorce, stepmom situation, etc. What should I do?" Well, what do you think she should do? If you got this letter, you might be thinking about how painful infidelity is. Or maybe about how especially painful it is here because of her experience growing up with her father. And like me, you'd probably have some empathy for this woman, and you might even have some, how should I put this nicely, let's just call them "not-so-positive" feelings for her husband. Now, those are the kinds of things that go through my mind too, when I'm reading these letters in my inbox. But I have to be really careful when I respond to these letters because I know that every letter I get is actually just a story written by a specific author. And that another version of this story also exists. It always does. And I know this because if I've learned anything as a therapist, it's that we are all unreliable narrators of our own lives. I am. You are. And so is everyone you know. Which I probably shouldn't have told you because now you're not going to believe my TED Talk. Look, I don't mean that we purposely mislead. Most of what people tell me is absolutely true, just from their current points of view. Depending on what they emphasize or minimize, what they leave in, what they leave out, what they see and want me to see, they tell their stories in a particular way. The psychologist Jerome Bruner described this beautifully -- he said, "To tell a story is, inescapably, to take a moral stance." All of us walk around with stories about our lives. Why choices were made, why things went wrong, why we treated someone a certain way -- because obviously, they deserved it -- why someone treated us a certain way -- even though, obviously, we didn't. Stories are the way we make sense of our lives. But what happens when the stories we tell are misleading or incomplete or just wrong? Well, instead of providing clarity, these stories keep us stuck. We assume that our circumstances shape our stories. But what I found time and again in my work is that the exact opposite happens. The way we narrate our lives shapes what they become. That's the danger of our stories, because they can really mess us up, but it's also their power. Because what it means is that if we can change our stories, then we can change our lives. And today, I want to show you how. Now, I told you I'm a therapist, and I really am, I'm not being an unreliable narrator. But if I'm, let's say, on an airplane, and someone asks what I do, I usually say I'm an editor. And I say that partly because if I say I'm a therapist, I always get some awkward response, like, "Oh, a therapist. Are you going to psychoanalyze me?" And I'm thinking, "A : no, and B: why would I do that here? If I said I was a gynecologist, would you ask if I were about to give you a pelvic exam?" But the main reason I say I'm an editor is because it's true. Now, it's the job of all therapists to help people edit, but what's interesting about my specific role as Dear Therapist is that when I edit, I'm not just editing for one person. I'm trying to teach a whole group of readers how to edit, using one letter each week as the example. So I'm thinking about things like, "What material is extraneous?" "Is the protagonist moving forward or going in circles, are the supporting characters important or are they a distraction?" "Do the plot points reveal a theme?" And what I've noticed is that most people's stories tend to circle around two key themes. The first is freedom, and the second is change. And when I edit, those are the themes that I start with. So, let's take a look at freedom for a second. Our stories about freedom go like this: we believe, in general, that we have an enormous amount of freedom. Except when it comes to the problem at hand, in which case, suddenly, we feel like we have none. Many of our stories are about feeling trapped, right? We feel imprisoned by our families, our jobs, our relationships, our pasts. Sometimes, we even imprison ourselves with a narrative of self-flagellation -- I know you guys all know these stories. The "everyone's life is better than mine" story, courtesy of social media. The "I'm an impostor" story, the "I'm unlovable" story, the "nothing will ever work out for me" story. The "when I say, 'Hey, Siri, ' and she doesn't answer, that means she hates me" story. I see you, see, I'm not the only one. The woman who wrote me that letter, she also feels trapped. If she stays with her husband, she'll never trust him again, but if she leaves, her children will suffer. Now, there's a cartoon that I think is a perfect example of what's really going on in these stories. The cartoon shows a prisoner shaking the bars, desperately trying to get out. But on the right and the left, it's open. No bars. The prisoner isn't in jail. That's most of us. We feel completely trapped, stuck in our emotional jail cells. But we don't walk around the bars to freedom because we know there's a catch. Freedom comes with responsibility. And if we take responsibility for our role in the story, we might just have to change. And that's the other common theme that I see in our stories: change. Those stories sound like this: a person says, "I want to change." But what they really mean is, "I want another character in the story to change." Therapists describe this dilemma as: "If the queen had balls, she'd be the king." I mean -- It makes no sense, right? Why wouldn't we want the protagonist, who's the hero of the story, to change? Well, it might be because change, even really positive change, involves a surprising amount of loss. Loss of the familiar. Even if the familiar is unpleasant or utterly miserable, at least we know the characters and setting and plot, right down to the recurring dialogue in this story. "You never do the laundry!" "I did it last time!" "Oh, yeah? When?" There's something oddly comforting about knowing exactly how the story is going to go every single time. To write a new chapter is to venture into the unknown. It's to stare at a blank page. And as any writer will tell you, there's nothing more terrifying than a blank page. But here's the thing. Once we edit our story, the next chapter becomes much easier to write. We talk so much in our culture about getting to know ourselves. But part of getting to know yourself is to unknow yourself. To let go of the one version of the story you've been telling yourself so that you can live your life, and not the story that you've been telling yourself about your life. And that's how we walk around those bars. So I want to go back to the letter from the woman, about the affair. She asked me what she should do. Now, I have this word taped up in my office: ultracrepidarianism. The habit of giving advice or opinions outside of one's knowledge or competence. It's a great word, right? You can use it in all different contexts, I'm sure you will be using it after this TED Talk. I use it because it reminds me that as a therapist, I can help people to sort out what they want to do, but I can't make their life choices for them. Only you can write your story, and all you need are some tools. So what I want to do is I want to edit this woman's letter together, right here, as a way to show how we can all revise our stories. And I want to start by asking you to think of a story that you're telling yourself right now that might not be serving you well. It might be about a circumstance you're experiencing, it might be about a person in your life, it might even be about yourself. And I want you to look at the supporting characters. Who are the people who are helping you to uphold the wrong version of this story? For instance, if the woman who wrote me that letter told her friends what happened, they would probably offer her what's called "idiot compassion." Now, in idiot compassion, we go along with the story, we say, "You're right, that's so unfair," when a friend tells us that he didn't get the promotion he wanted, even though we know this has happened several times before because he doesn't really put in the effort, and he probably also steals office supplies. We say, "Yeah, you're right, he's a jerk," when a friend tells us that her boyfriend broke up with her, even though we know that there are certain ways she tends to behave in relationships, like the incessant texting or the going through his drawers, that tend to lead to this outcome. We see the problem, it's like, if a fight breaks out in every bar you're going to, it might be you. In order to be good editors, we need to offer wise compassion, not just to our friends, but to ourselves. This is what's called -- I think the technical term might be -- "delivering compassionate truth bombs." And these truth bombs are compassionate, because they help us to see what we've left out of the story. The truth is, we don't know if this woman's husband is having an affair, or why their sex life changed two years ago, or what those late-night phone calls are really about. And it might be that because of her history, she's writing a singular story of betrayal, but there's probably something else that she's not willing to let me, in her letter, or maybe even herself, to see. It's like that guy who's taking a Rorschach test. You all know what Rorschach tests are? A psychologist shows you some ink blots, they look like that, and asks, "What do you see?" So the guy looks at his ink blot and he says, "Well, I definitely don't see blood." And the examiner says, "Alright, tell me what else you definitely don't see." In writing, this is called point of view. What is the narrator not willing to see? So, I want to read you one more letter. And it goes like this. I need help with my wife. Lately, everything I do irritates her, even small things, like the noise I make when I chew. At breakfast, I noticed that she even tries to secretly put extra milk in my granola so it won't be as crunchy." "I feel like she became critical of me after my father died two years ago. I was very close with him, and her father left when she was young, so she couldn't relate to what I was going through. There's a friend at work whose father died a few months ago, and who understands my grief. I wish I could talk to my wife like I talk to my friend, but I feel like she barely tolerates me now. How can I get my wife back?" OK. So, what you probably picked up on is that this is the same story I read you earlier, just told from another narrator's point of view. Her story was about a husband who's cheating, his story is about a wife who can't understand his grief. But what's remarkable, is that for all of their differences, what both of these stories are about is a longing for connection. And if we can get out of the first-person narration and write the story from another character's perspective, suddenly that other character becomes much more sympathetic, and the plot opens up. That's the hardest step in the editing process, but it's also where change begins. What would happen if you looked at your story and wrote it from another person's point of view? What would you see now from this wider perspective? That's why, when I see people who are depressed, I sometimes say, "You are not the best person to talk to you about you right now," because depression distorts our stories in a very particular way. It narrows our perspectives. The same is true when we feel lonely or hurt or rejected. We create all kinds of stories, distorted through a very narrow lens that we don't even know we're looking through. And then, we've effectively become our own fake-news broadcasters. I have a confession to make. I wrote the husband's version of the letter I read you. You have no idea how much time I spent debating between granola and pita chips, by the way. I wrote it based on all of the alternative narratives that I've seen over the years, not just in my therapy practice, but also in my column. When it's happened that two people involved in the same situation have written to me, unbeknownst to the other, and I have two versions of the same story sitting in my inbox. That really has happened. I don't know what the other version of this woman's letter is, but I do know this: she has to write it. Because with a courageous edit, she'll write a much more nuanced version of her letter that she wrote to me. Even if her husband is having an affair of any kind -- and maybe he is -- she doesn't need to know what the plot is yet. Because just by virtue of doing an edit, she'll have so many more possibilities for what the plot can become. Now, sometimes it happens that I see people who are really stuck, and they're really invested in their stuckness. We call them help-rejecting complainers. I'm sure you know people like this. They're the people who, when you try to offer them a suggestion, they reject it with, "Yeah, no, that will never work, because ..." "Yeah, no, that's impossible, because I can't do that." "Yeah, I really want more friends, but people are just so annoying." What they're really rejecting is an edit to their story of misery and stuckness. And so, with these people, I usually take a different approach. And what I do is I say something else. I say to them, "We're all going to die." I bet you're really glad I'm not your therapist right now. Because they look back at me the way you're looking back at me right now, with this look of utter confusion. But then I explain that there's a story that gets written about all of us, eventually. It's called an obituary. And I say that instead of being authors of our own unhappiness, we get to shape these stories while we're still alive. We get to be the hero and not the victim in our stories, we get to choose what goes on the page that lives in our minds and shapes our realities. I tell them that life is about deciding which stories to listen to and which ones need an edit. And that it's worth the effort to go through a revision because there's nothing more important to the quality of our lives than the stories we tell ourselves about them. I say that when it comes to the stories of our lives, we should be aiming for our own personal Pulitzer Prize. Now, most of us aren't help-rejecting complainers, or at least we don't believe we are. But it's a role that is so easy to slip into when we feel anxious or angry or vulnerable. So the next time you're struggling with something, remember, we're all going to die. And then pull out your editing tools and ask yourself: what do I want my story to be? And then, go write your masterpiece. Thank you. (Applause)

- How YouTube Changed My Life (In Less Than a Year): Kind: captions Language: en 12 months ago, I finally did a thing that I'd been thinking about for years. I pressed record on my very first YouTube video. Since that first video went live, I made a commitment to stay as consistent as possible for as long as I could, and I managed to post two videos per week for the better part of a year. Now, I wish I could tell you that it's been nothing but a whirlwind of growth and fulfillment, but it hasn't. And while some videos performed well, most did not. And after a year, I'm still learning how to handle that. I've been through phases of obsessing over the YouTube analytics and consuming endless YouTube growth content, trying to crack the elusive YouTube algorithm. But my only real insight after 12 months and over 100 videos is that the only way to grow on YouTube is to make better content. But you already know that from the outside looking in, you'll see my subscribers and think that I've done something right in order to be here. And if I have, I'm not exactly sure what that is. You see, the thing about growing on YouTube is that it's often very messy and unplanned, riddled with frustration and doubt, only to be temporarily alleviated by rapid sporadic growth. Nonetheless, over the last 12 months, I have fumbled my way into building a small but growing business around my YouTube channel. I've joined the YouTube partner program, and I'm now managing a community of like-minded midlife content creators. But despite all of this, in many other ways, it still feels like my first day at school. This is my one-year time capsule. What I've learned trying to do YouTube full-time, and a reflection on how YouTube has changed my life in less than a year. I've always admired YouTubers, their creativity, their courage, and the value that they freely give to the world. This platform is actually really magical when you think about it. Never before has humanity been able to connect like this. But I've always been the quiet one, reserved, never seeking the spotlight. In school, I rarely raised my hand. And like many of you, I just assumed that YouTube would forever be this thing that I watched, not something that I did. But then something shifted. Sitting in front of my computer, where I spent far more time than is healthy. At 44 years old and approaching almost 20 years in corporate America, I realized I was living the same day over and over again, and I was running out of time. When I got more serious about starting on YouTube, I took a camera confidence boot camp. The curriculum was great, but the real lesson came from a throwaway comment by a 65-year-old classmate. I watched one of his videos, and I asked him how he was so natural on camera, and he responded to me saying, "Mate, I'm a 65-year-old bloke. I don't have any more time to care about what other people think, so just be who you are. And that comment really stuck with me along with watching other inspirational videos from Alexa Saranoa and Rob the Maritimer. That comment helped get me over the hump to say, "F it" and just get going with YouTube. I wasn't vocal about starting. I told people, but I kept my expectations low. After all, very few people ever succeed on YouTube. And from the outside perspective, it probably seems like an awful waste of time. I'm not a particularly interesting person. I don't have that quintessential YouTube energy, charisma, or playfulness. But I had convinced myself of one thing, that I was a halfway decent teacher. And if I could capitalize on that one skill, then I might have a shot. But there was only one problem. I didn't know anything about the subject that I'd be teaching. So, what did I devote my channel to? Well, how to start and grow on YouTube. The irony was not lost on me. Here I was about to make my very first video about making your very first video. And my very first video sucked. No, I mean it. Like quite literally, I put it sucked in the thumbnail. It was my attempt at explaining what it's like to try and start and grow a YouTube channel when you have no idea what you're doing. I shot it on my iPhone with a mic that I had picked up from Amazon. And three takes later, I settled on the least terrible version. Finally, after years of thinking about it and months of heavy research mixed with procrastination, I hit publish. The relief was instant. The hardest part was behind me. And even 12 months later, I still believe that to be true. But something unexpected happened. The video started to take off. I got 150 views on my first day, 700 by the end of my third, 50 subscribers on my channel. I couldn't believe what was happening. But YouTube, of course, had other plans. The excitement quickly fizzled. Every follow-up video for months barely registered. Most videos struggled to get 100 views and only a few more subscribers trickled in. I went from feeling like I overcame this giant fear and I cracked the YouTube algorithm to feeling like I was a one-hit wonder who just got lucky on day one. The roller coaster ride was on. I'd been warned that this would take years to master. So, I made a commitment. Two videos per week no matter what. I was still working my 9 to5 and I kept that up for a good 4 months posting twice weekly. I wish I could tell you that I'd found some secret system. But I'm just as sloppy and half-hazard now as I was back then. Only back then I had less time, so I was scrambling constantly. And here's the thing about not knowing the subject matter in your niche. when you're teaching how to grow on YouTube, but you have no subscribers yourself and you can't exactly make videos about how to get views or how to get monetized. So, I talked about what I had my fumbling experience learning the YouTube studio, stumbling through thumbnails and SEO. I made terrible videos about those topics and probably gave out a lot of really bad advice. But YouTube was like this entire world opening up to me. It was really exciting, but it was also like getting covered by an avalanche on the bunny hill. 3 months in, I hit a wall. Burnt out from endless editing sessions, falling behind on my posting schedule. I was in a bit of a panic mode, frustrated, desperate, coming up on a deadline with no script, no plan, no thumbnail, no title. I went to the roof and recorded my very first video, just speaking completely off the riff. And to my surprise, it actually performed quite well. I was afraid to post it initially because it was too raw, too vulnerable, and almost too self- serving, but I was pleasantly surprised when I saw so many people connecting with it. 1,000 views, 3,000 views, 6,000 views. And for a channel of my size, that was amazing. It was a breakthrough of a plateau that I'd been stuck on. And this one video helped propel me to almost 1,000 subscribers. The lesson that stuck with me ever since is that authenticity trumps polish every single time. But despite this breakthrough, I was still scrambling. Videos were thrown together at the last minute, recording secretly in my kitchen when my partner wasn't home because I was terrified that she'd hear me stumbling over my words for hours sounding like an idiot. But somehow I made it all work. Despite my insecurities, the channel was growing. Sliver of light began to emerge. a slight indication that growing on YouTube was actually possible. I invested in a YouTube coach to help my own progression. 4 months in, barely knowing what I was doing, she dropped a bombshell on me. She encouraged me to start my own coaching program. I thought she was crazy. Who am I to coach newer people when I myself am barely figuring things out? But I quietly added a calendarly link to my video descriptions and started mentioning it in my videos. This decision to act before I was ready really paid off. Within a week, I had my first client book a paid coaching sessions with me. A week later, another client booked. And then I made an even crazier decision. I had been secretly sitting on an impulse for months, leaving my company of almost 20 years. I know, I know. Never quit your day job for YouTube until you hit certain financial milestones. This is the advice I would give and I will still give to anyone who asks that question. But I'm terrible at following my own advice. It wasn't that I was unhappy with my old company. I loved working there, but something was missing. Purpose, fulfillment, desire. 7 years of intentional saving and living below my means meant that I had earned myself a bit of a financial cushion, one to two years if I needed it. At 44, with a mortgage and only a few other obligations, I felt like the risk was manageable. Coupled with the recent growth on my channel and my first few coaching clients, I felt empowered to take a chance and bet on myself. I put in my two weeks, and in my fifth month of doing YouTube, I was now full-time. The weight of this decision hit immediately. After nearly two decades of having a boss, I was suddenly responsible for everything. success, failure, the hours I kept, the tasks I worked on, all of it was on me. What I didn't expect was how much money would mess with my head. For the first time in years, I was thinking about expenses like a broke college student. The guilt of putting this pressure on my partner was starting to crash down on me. Thankfully, I had her love and support and oh, yeah, her health insurance, too. By the end of the year, I had picked up a few more clients. My single session offer evolved into a 90-day program. And by January, I had a half dozen clients, but I still couldn't see how this would become a full-time income. The clock was ticking. I had 12 to 18 months to figure this out. Otherwise, it would be back to the 9 to5. The clock is still ticking. My videos became a mix of strategy, tutorials, and the more raw and real sharing my stumbling journey. iPhone tutorials, thumbnail tips, behind the scenes of how I record videos. But I found a bit of a sweet spot. The emotional side of YouTube. Turns out a lot of people need help pushing through self-doubt and the limiting beliefs that we pile on ourselves. Something that I know all too well. In January, I launched a school community for my coaching clients. It was a place to network, ask questions, and support each other. And then I had an idea. Why not open it up to everyone? I started mentioning in my videos and midlife YouTubers began to trickle in. Suddenly, I can start to feel the ground shift beneath me. I started to sense real interest in what I was doing and I started to fall in love with this community. I could spend all day in there talking to people, answering questions, creating tutorials, just trying to be helpful. It became my greatest joy from this entire YouTube venture so far. These people from all over the world who show up for one another. It's incredible to see and it's heartwarming to know that I had a hand in making that happen. The channel kept growing with scattered wins among the usual mediocre videos. Still posting twice weekly but now hosting calls in the community as well and getting more serious about my email list. Then came the milestone. By the end of January, I had joined the YouTube partner program which was now exactly 6 months on YouTube. I was ecstatic well for exactly 3 days before I realized I had to keep going. Getting monetized doesn't mean you're instantly rich. My first few days earned me just a couple of bucks. The real gut punch, though, is the realization that the mountain you've been climbing doesn't stop at monetization. In fact, the true reward for getting monetized is seeing those much taller mountains that lay ahead of you beyond. The sprint was finally catching up with me. 6 months of twice weekly posting had me officially burnt out. I was dreading video creation, procrastinating, and then scrambling last minute, missing deadlines and beating myself up for the inconsistency. I'd spend the next 6 weeks in a lull, struggling to post, riddled with self-doubt and unable to see a way forward. At my lowest, I stumbled upon another YouTuber named Dea and a video she made about a book called The Dip by Seth Goden. Reading this book and immersing myself in trying to help my clients single-handedly got me out of this rut. I got really clear about two things during that time. I needed a new goal now that I've reached monetization and I needed a simpler way to get there. I told myself that if I could just make enough money from YouTube to cover all of my monthly expenses, then I could just hang out there indefinitely until I figured out how to make more money. This became my new mountain to climb. And the way I would get there would be to start each week with one simple question. Who can I help? And what problem can I solve? It is all too easy to over complicate things, to try and do too much and to be terrible at all of it. Accepting that you can only do so much and simplifying your approach is the real key to consistency. And consistency is how you win on YouTube. And it was this approach that got me back into the right headsp space. Over the next few months, things were slowgoing. I spotted a video from another channel about how much they made after being monetized for 90 days. This video at the time had 1.2 2 million views. By late April, I was 90 days monetized myself, and I decided to make my own version. Perfect for a YouTube growth channel. Or so I thought. My video took off and blew up. It became my best performer by miles, shooting me past 5,000 and now 6,000 subscribers and dramatically increasing my AdSense earnings. One video really can set things off. But here's the warning. Going viral is a double-edged sword. Views are great, don't get me wrong, but if the new viewers don't care about your other content, subscriber count becomes meaningless. And to some extent, this is happening right now on my channel. So, consider yourself warned. As I approach 12 months, I'm busier than I've ever been, but in the best possible way. Being your own boss is terrifying. I'm constantly wonder if I'm doing enough or wasting my time on the wrong things. There's nobody there to tell you. And that's both liberating and paralyzing at the same time. But here's some great news. I've recently joined another mentorship program with others in the early stages of their YouTube business journey. And within just a few days of joining this group has challenged me yet again to take messy action and push beyond what I thought I was capable of. The nudge worked and resulted in me making enough sales to pay for the program I was just 2 weeks into. This wasn't just a financial breakthrough, but a shattering of a belief that I had about myself that I wasn't worth paying for. I never would have known the answer to this question had I not asked it to begin with. So, what questions are you not asking because you're afraid of the answer? YouTube is nothing if not prolonged bouts of frustration interrupted by meaningful but sometimes short breakthroughs. These feel like genuine level ups, but you have to seek them out. And that often means failing many, many times before something good actually happens. So, how exactly have I changed? Well, most days I'm genuinely jumping out of bed to start the day. I've never had that in my corporate job. I have YouTube friends now. Many were clients who became like brothers and sisters. Even my old coach still puts up with me bugging her regularly. She's the best. I'm more physically active than I've been in the last 10 years of my life. I'm sleeping better. I'm reading more. I'm no longer scared to record myself and I become quite numb to the sound of my own voice, which is a good thing. But I do constantly worry that I'm not providing enough value or working hard enough on my videos. I am admittedly more stressed out than I was in my day job. But at least it's tied to building something unmistakably mine. I can't put into words how freeing it is to be building with your own two hands. Even with the cloudiness of uncertainty looming overhead, every day I'm at the mercy of the algorithm I still haven't figured out. Every day I could choose to sleep in and accomplish nothing. And that temptation does arise. But luckily, I've put myself in a situation where clients depend on me and that motivates me to keep showing up. One word of caution though, in the spirit of transparency, I haven't been the best partner to my significant other. I know that I'm not fully present for her and I know that this is affecting our shared happiness. I need to do better because if I don't, this dream life that I'm trying to build for us will be lived alone. And that's an unbearable thought I refuse to explore. So, if you're anything like me, prone to tunnel vision and obsession, make it a point to regularly step back. Remember who you're doing all of this for to begin with. It's hard to know what the future looks like, and I still can't imagine how I'll get back to a full-time income. But I'm hellbent on learning. This is the longest I've ever stuck with anything I've ever tried in my life, and I refuse to quit now. If you're thinking about starting a channel, here is my advice. Figure out why you want to do YouTube. And once you know why, just start. YouTube is a learn as you go kind of hobby or job, if you want to call it that. People appreciate the build with me approach and if you want to learn a new skill and share it as you learn it, you can just follow my example. You will never be perfect. In fact, you are very much meant not to be. Those imperfections will be your endearing personality traits that your viewers will come to love. When you are no longer afraid of who you are, the right people will surround you. And if you're one of my community members and you've made it this far in the video, thanks for being here and thanks for giving me the opportunity to build alongside you. See you on the next call.

- Leverage creator partnerships with Shopify Collabs: Kind: captions Language: de Unternehmen suchen neue, kreative Marketing-Lösungen und Creators und Influencer:innen gehören heute zu den effektivsten Tools zum Ansprechen neuer Zielgruppen. Deshalb haben wir Shopify Collabs eingeführt. Ein kostenlos installierbares All-in-one-Tool zum Entdecken und Aufbauen einer Creator-Community. Du kannst Produkte authentisch präsentieren und den Umsatz steigern, alles vom Shopify-Adminbereich aus. Shopify Collabs ist leicht einzurichten und zu nutzen. Installiere es zuerst aus dem Shopify App Store. Mit unserer Setup-Anleitung kannst du leicht eine markentreue Bewerbungsseite für deine Community erstellen. Die App sammelt wichtige Informationen wie E-Mails und Social-Media-Konten. Du kannst unter "Discover" (Entdecken) nach Creators suchen, die zu deiner Marke passen. Nutze dazu Filter für Standort, Keywords, Hashtags, Preise für Social-Media-Engagements und Follower-Anzahl. Egal ob du über "Discover" (Entdecken) neue Creators ansprichst oder Anfragen über Social Media oder E-Mail erhältst, können sie sich auf deine Einladung hin auf dieser Seite bewerben, und du kannst alles über Collabs verwalten. Unter "Applicants" (Bewerber) kannst du Bewerbungen von Creators prüfen und annehmen. Wenn du Creators als Mitglieder bestätigst, kannst du Gruppen, Geschenke und Affiliate-Ebenen einrichten, um Beziehungen effizient zu verwalten. Sortiere Creators nach Thema oder demografischen Gruppen, um Kampagnen effektiv zu planen und umzusetzen. Mit Gruppen ist es einfacher, Geschenke zu senden und Affiliate-Programme in großem Umfang mittels Serienaktionen zu verwalten. Du kannst Creators in deiner Community direkt über Collabs Geschenke senden, damit sie deine Produkte testen und authentische Inhalte erstellen können. Wähle Produkte, die du anbieten willst, und sende sie direkt über die App. Creators können ihr Geschenk, Größe und Farben wählen und ihre gewünschte Lieferadresse eingeben. Collabs automatisiert die zeitaufwändigen Geschenkschritte, damit du weniger Zeit mit Tabellen oder E-Mails verbringen musst. Motiviere Creators dazu, deine Produkte zu bewerben, indem du sie zu Affiliates machst, damit sie exklusive Rabatte gewähren und Provisionen für Verkäufe verdienen können. Du kannst mehrere Affiliate-Ebenen einrichten, um Creators schnell vorkonfigurierte Rabatte und Provisionssätze zuzuweisen und diese problemlos zu verwalten. Wenn Creators Provisionen verdienen, kannst du direkt in der App die Conversion-Rate sehen und Zahlungen verwalten. Überwache und analysiere die Leistung deines Affiliate-Programms mit Berichterstattungstools, um es mit der Zeit zu optimieren. Authentische Beziehungen mit Creators aufzubauen, die deine Produkte mögen, ist ein guter Weg, deine Marke neuen Zielgruppen vorzustellen und den Umsatz zu steigern. Schaffe dir noch heute eine Community mit Shopify Collabs. Wir sind gespannt auf deine nächste Kooperation.

- Building Customer Loyalty: Loyalty as a Behavior | Knowledgecity.com: Kind: captions Language: en One of the first things to understand about loyalty is that it is a form of human behavior just like sorrow, sympathy, or regret. And like these other forms of expression, loyalty is influenced by factors that lie deep within our subconscious minds. We may think of loyalty as a very conscious decision or reaction. But in reality, it is very much driven by subconscious factors. A brief explanation in psychology is warranted here. Humans are endowed with a capability to store many things in memory and recall them instantaneously. Sometimes we intentionally place something in memory for later recall, such as the directions to a place we may visit more than once. But more often, we experience something and our brain automatically stores it in memory. When it does, it also stores information about the effect associated with it. Did we like it, dislike it, fear it? Often the stronger the effect, the stronger the ability to recall the item. That's why things that did not make much of an impression on us are more difficult to remember. This effect tagging occurs for people we meet, places we visit, experiences we have, and many other things we encounter. Let's say you meet someone new. Initial impressions are formed very quickly. So the person and the associated effect get placed in memory. The next time you encounter that person, your brain automatically provides you with the memory and the effect it originally stored. These are both conscious and unconscious forms of loyalty. The conscious form might be a local groceryer where you realize that the local market is more expensive and less likely to carry everything you need. But you make it a point to shop there as a conscious decision to support business in your neighborhood. Unconscious loyalty might be a website you go to for certain things that has now become a habit that you always try first without even thinking about it. As a behavior, loyalty can be a very complex variable with different types and degrees for different things. Customer loyalties to different stores, brands, or businesses vary extensively. In addition, unconscious loyalty is somewhat sticky and that once established, our mind will automatically keep going back to that until you override that unconscious thought with a new plan. not an easy proposition and then the new plan becomes somewhat sticky. There are also varying degrees of loyalty from weak to strong and we frequently share or split our loyalties between two or more products or businesses. For example, there may be two or three different gas stations we frequent depending on price, convenience, and other factors. Other situations may exist where you enjoy using two different vendors and are equally satisfied with both providers. You might go as far as to intentionally split your business as a way of being at least partially loyal to each. In general, moving from one vendor to another is very easy substitution. Loyalty is somewhat weaker. But when there are barriers to changing, like filling out paperwork for a new account or driving much further to find it, then loyalty is stronger because there is a cost to switching, either inconvenience or some other factor. One final point to mention here about loyalty as a behavior is that loyalty and satisfaction are related, but not always the same. You can be loyal to a business without being totally satisfied, especially if there are few or no alternatives, or if the alternatives are much less convenient. On the other hand, you can be satisfied with the business but not act particularly loyal if you can get the same item or service elsewhere at a much lower price.
